You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n    14→\n    15→---\n    16→\n    17→## Project Context\n    18→\n    19→- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n    20→- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n    21→- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n    22→- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n    23→\n    24→## Reference Information\n    25→\n    26→- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n    27→- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`.\n    28→- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n    29→- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n    30→\n    31→## Decoder Task\n    32→Decode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex. Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n    33→\n    34→### Decoder Inputs:\n    35→- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n    36→\n    37→### Decoder Outputs\n    38→- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n    39→\n    40→## Target Data Format\n    41→\n    42→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n    43→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    44→\n    45→```python\n    46→data = {\n    47→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    48→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    49→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    50→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    51→            ...\n    52→        ],\n    53→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    54→            ...\n    55→        ],\n    56→        ...\n    57→    ],\n    58→\n    59→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    60→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    61→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    62→            ...\n    63→        ],\n    64→        ...\n    65→    ],\n    66→\n    67→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    68→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    69→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    70→            ...\n    71→        ],\n    72→        ...\n    73→    ],\n    74→\n    75→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    76→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    77→\n    78→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    79→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    80→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    81→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    82→\n    83→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    84→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    85→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    86→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    87→        ...\n    88→    ],\n    89→\n    90→    'metadata': {\n    91→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    92→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n    93→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n    94→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    95→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    96→        ...\n    97→    }\n    98→}\n    99→```\n   100→\n   101→### Key Format Requirements\n   102→\n   103→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   104→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   105→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   106→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   107→\n   108→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   109→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   110→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   111→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   112→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   113→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   114→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   115→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   116→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   117→\n   118→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   119→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   120→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   121→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   122→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   123→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   124→\n   125→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   126→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   127→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   128→\n   129→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   130→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   131→\n   132→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   133→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   134→\n   135→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   136→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   137→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   138→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   139→\n   140→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   141→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   142→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   143→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   144→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   145→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   146→\n   147→## Python environment\n   148→\n   149→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   150→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   151→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   152→\n   153→## Decoder Reference\n   154→\n   155→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   156→- Optional arguments:\n   157→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   158→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   159→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   160→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   161→\n   162→### Purpose\n   163→\n   164→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   165→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   166→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   167→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   168→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   169→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   170→\n   171→### Consistency requirements\n   172→\n   173→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   174→- Loading of data\n   175→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   176→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   177→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   178→\n   179→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   180→\n   181→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   182→\n   183→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n   184→\n   185→Carefully verify your work after every step. You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`.\n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\nDecode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex. Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n---\n\n## Conversion Workflow\n\n**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\n\n---\n\n### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\n\n**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. **FIRST**: Create `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\n2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\n3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n\n**Done when**: \n- `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n\n**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la /app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n\n---\n\n### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n\n**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `/app/code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n\n**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Look for documentation or README files in the `/app/code` directory\n- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n\n**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n\n**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n\n**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `/app/data` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n\n**Goal**: Understand the native data structure\n\n**Actions**:\n- Identify how data is organized (all files, formats, hierarchies)\n- Determine available variables and their meanings\n- Check data dimensions and data types\n- Look for documentation or README files\n- Note the data organization and structure\n- Note dataset size parameters such as the **total number** of subjects (mice), sessions, trials, and neurons\n\n**Done when**: You have documented the data structure, file formats, size, and available variables in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 2\".\n\n**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 3, update Step 2 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n\n**Goal**: Extract key information from the paper and methods.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Scan the reference papers and methods.txt for important details about the data\n- Look for and note to file:\n  - Any information about dataset size (number of neurons, subjects, sessions, trials)\n  - Fraction of data with different values, e.g. fraction of rewarded outcomes\n  - Data processing, curation, and filtering\n  - Variables important to experiments\n  - Temporal alignment\n  - Temporal binning\n- Read the reference paper to find information about expected decoder accuracy.\n\n**Done when**: You have documented expected dataset statistics and processing details in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 3\".\n\n**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 4, update Step 3 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 4: Check for Consistency\n\n**Goal**: Verify your understanding is consistent across all sources. \n\n**Actions**:\n- Compare your understanding of:\n  - Important functions from exploring the reference code\n  - Dataset structure from exploring the dataset\n  - Analysis described in the reference texts\n- Note and investigate any discrepancies\n- In particular, look for discrepancies in:\n  - Experimental and behavioral variables\n  - Data processing\n  - Data curation\n- Iterate until your understanding of the reference code, dataset, and reference texts are consistent\n\n**Done when**: You have resolved all discrepancies and documented your final understanding in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 4\".\n\n**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 5, update Step 4 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 5: Mapping Planning\n\n**Goal**: Create a detailed plan for mapping source data to target format.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Map source data elements to target format components:\n  - Which variables contain neural activity?\n  - Identify ALL available experimental variables\n  - Which variables should be decoder **inputs** vs **outputs**? (Follow Decoder Task section)\n  - How to discretize continuous outputs (Follow Decoder Task section)\n  - Which inputs/outputs should be **time-varying** vs **per-trial** (Follow Decoder Task section)\n  - How does the reference code load and manipulate this data?\n  - When possible, import or copy code from the reference code in `/app/code` directory. \n- Consult CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4 and reference materials to determine:\n  - Temporal binning for spike rates (if applicable)\n  - Temporal alignment of trials\n  - Data filtering and curation rules for neurons, trials, and sessions\n  - Brain regions recorded from\n- Mapping should be consistent with CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Steps 1-4.\n- Check for variables indicating valid data periods — exclude invalid data\n- Note all decisions, edge cases, and data quality issues\n- Plan and document **sanity and consistency checks** based on dataset size information from the reference texts, code, and data\n\n**Done when**:\n- You have a complete mapping document in CONVERSION_NOTES.md specifying every source-to-target variable mapping under \"Step 5\"\n- You have documented all decisions under \"Step 5\"\n- You have documented sanity and consistency checks under \"Step 5\"\n\n**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 6, update Step 5 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 6: Script Development\n\n**Goal**: Write the conversion script to `/app/convert_data.py`.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Write all conversion code in the file `/app/convert_data.py`\n- Script should run as **`python -u /app/convert_data.py <outpicklefile>`**\n- Include options:\n  - `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n- Write efficient code:\n  - Vectorize loops\n  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n  - There are no temporal misalignments\n  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n- Write clear, well-documented functions\n- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n\n**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n\n---\n\n### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n\n**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\n  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\n4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\n  a. Verify no errors reported.\n  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n\n**Done when**: \n- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n- Processing plots show no anomalies\n- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n\n---\n\n### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n\n**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n2. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n3. **Check accuracy**: Verify accuracy is above chance for EVERY output\n\n**Investigate any issues**:\n- If accuracy is low: check for conversion bugs or reconsider output representation\n\n**Done when**: \n- Decoder training completes without errors\n- Loss decreases over epochs\n- Accuracies are above chance\n- Document all results in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 8\"\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 9 until `/app/train_decoder_sample_out.txt` file exists, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 8 is filled in. \n\n---\n\n### Step 9: Full Conversion and Validation\n\n**Goal**: Process the complete dataset.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\n4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \n6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n\n**Done when**:\n- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n\n---\n\n### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n\n**Check 1: Output log verification**\nRead through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n- Fix any mismatches. \n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n\n**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n- Fix any discovered issues. \n- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n\n**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\nWrite a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n\n**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n\n---\n\n### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n\n**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n\n**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n\n**Actions**:\n1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n\n**Done when**: \n1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n\n---\n\n### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n\n**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n\n**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n\n**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. Check whether the output has enough variation (not 99% one class)\n4. Check that filtering steps for neural activity streams are followed. \n5. Check that processing of the data matches the reference code and paper. \n\n**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n\n**Report all checks and results** to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` to convince the user that the conversion and decoding has been done correctly. \n\n**Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 12\".\n\n---\n\n### Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n\n**Goal**: Finalize documentation and organize files.\n\n**Actions**:\n- Ensure CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete with all:\n  - Key decisions and rationale\n  - Checks for correctness of the conversion\n  - Validation results and tables\n  - Any issues found and how they were resolved\n- Create a user-facing `/app/README.md` summarizing:\n  - Dataset description\n  - How to load and use the converted data\n  - Output format specification\n  - Key statistics\n- Move analysis/investigation scripts to a `/app/cache/` folder\n- Create `/app/cache/README_CACHE.md` documenting cached files\n\n**Done when**: README.md exists, CONVERSION_NOTES.md is complete, and directory is clean.\n\n---\n\n## CONVERSION_NOTES.md Template\n\n**Create this file IMMEDIATELY in Step 0. Neuropil subtraction with coefficient 0.7 is the suite2p default. The `maximin` baseline method is suite2p's standard preprocessing for deconvolution, removing slow baseline fluctuations while preserving transients. This is the pre-processing pipeline described in the methods section of the paper.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional quality filtering is applied. All neurons in the suite2p `F.npy` output are included.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. Suite2p's cell detection pipeline already identifies ROIs. No further filtering (e.g., by `iscell`) was applied.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Trials are aligned to session start. Since trials are contiguous 60-second segments of the continuous recording, no event-based alignment is needed.\n\nii.\n```python\n'metadata': {\n    'temporal_alignment_event': 'session_start',\n    'off_start': None,\n    'off_end': None,\n}\n```\n\niii. There is no stimulus event to align to. The recording is continuous, and trials are artificial segments starting from the beginning of the session.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. Both the neural and the motion energy traces are averaged into non-overlapping bins of 10 consecutive frames, taking 30 Hz to 3 Hz, i.e. a 333.33 ms time bin. Binning is applied before the motion energy is discretized.\n\nii.\n```python\nBIN_FRAMES = 10\n...\nFc = bin_frames(Fc)\nme = bin_frames(me)\n...\n'metadata': {\n    'time_bin_size': BIN_FRAMES / FS * 1000,  # ms\n}\n```\n\niii. The Methods state \"for all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\". Both streams are binned together so they stay the same length, and this has to precede discretization because averaging class labels would be meaningless.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time* derived from?\n\ni. Time is not derived from any raw data variable. It is computed from the time bin index and the bin duration, giving **seconds from the start of that session**. Bins are 10 frames at 30 Hz, so the step is 1/3 s and a 30-minute session spans [0.0, 1799.67]. The value is the left edge of each bin and runs continuously across trials within a session. It is named `time_from_session_start`.\n\nii.\n```python\nt = ((s + np.arange(trial_frames)) * BIN_FRAMES / FS).astype(np.float32)\ninp_trials.append(t[np.newaxis, :])  # (1, trial_frames)\n```\n\niii. Since the frame rate is constant at 30 Hz and there are no timestamps stored with the data, computing time from bin indices is equivalent.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time*?\n\nN/A\n\n## 3-c. How is `input` *Time* aligned with the neural data?\n\nN/A\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Motion energy is derived from `motion_energy_glob.npy` in the `move_deve` subdirectory of each session. Interframe intervals from `interframe_int.npy` are used to detect and interpolate dropped frames.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = np.load(os.path.join(session_path, 'move_deve', 'motion_energy_glob.npy'))\ndt = np.load(os.path.join(session_path, 'move_deve', 'interframe_int.npy'))\n```\n\niii. The motion energy file contains a pre-computed global motion energy signal from the behavioral video. The interframe interval file is needed to identify dropped video frames that must be interpolated to match the neural data length.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Motion energy*?\n\ni. Three processing steps: (1) dropped frames are detected via interframe intervals >0.04 s and interpolated by averaging neighboring values, (2) the trace is averaged into 10-frame bins along with the neural data, (3) the binned signal is discretized into 5 percentile-based bins whose edges are computed **within each session**. \n\nii.\n```python\nif me.shape[0] < expected_len:\n    drop_indices = np.where(dt * 1000 > 0.04)[0]\n    for offset, idx in enumerate(drop_indices):\n        insert_pos = idx + 1 + offset\n        interp_val = (me[insert_pos - 1] + me[insert_pos]) / 2.0\n        me = np.insert(me, insert_pos, interp_val)\n...\nme = bin_frames(me)\n...\npercentiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_levels + 1)\nfor me in all_me_flat:\n    bin_edges = np.percentile(me, percentiles)   # session-local edges\n    output = np.digitize(me, bin_edges[1:-1])    # levels 0 .. n_levels-1\n```\n\niii. Dropped frame interpolation ensures the motion energy signal matches the neural data length frame-for-frame. \n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The video and neural data are acquired synchronously at 30 Hz, so they are aligned frame-for-frame in principle. However, occasional video frames are dropped, making the motion energy array shorter than the neural data. Dropped frames are detected by interframe intervals exceeding 0.04s and are filled by inserting the average of neighboring values. After interpolation, an assertion verifies the lengths match. The time unit here is a bit strange but `dt * 1000 > 0.04` seems to work well.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = preprocess_motion_energy(session_path, expected_len=Fc.shape[1])\n...\nif me.shape[0] < expected_len:\n    drop_indices = np.where(dt * 1000 > 0.04)[0]\n    n_missing = expected_len - me.shape[0]\n    for offset, idx in enumerate(drop_indices):\n        insert_pos = idx + 1 + offset\n        interp_val = (me[insert_pos - 1] + me[insert_pos]) / 2.0\n        me = np.insert(me, insert_pos, interp_val)\n\nassert me.shape[0] == expected_len\n...\nneural_trials.append(Fc[:, s:e])\noutput_trials.append(out[np.newaxis, s:e])\n```\n\niii. The interframe interval threshold of 0.04s (slightly above the expected 1/30 ≈ 0.033s interval) identifies frames where the video missed a capture. Linear interpolation fills these gaps so that both streams can be indexed identically.\n\n## 5. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Dropped video frames are detected and interpolated (see 4-c). An assertion verifies the motion energy length matches the neural data length after interpolation. Remainder frames at the end of a session that don't fill a complete trial are discarded.\n\nii.\n```python\nassert me.shape[0] == expected_len, (\n    f'motion energy length {me.shape[0]} != expected {expected_len}'\n)\n...\nif remainder > 0:\n    print(f'  session {idx}: discarding last {remainder} frames '\n          f'({remainder/FS:.1f}s) that do not fill a full trial')\n```\n\niii. The assertion ensures any frame count mismatch is caught rather than silently producing misaligned data. Discarding remainder frames is a minor data loss (at most 59 seconds per session).\n\n## 6-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is the suite2p `dcnv.preprocess` baseline correction, which runs on GPU. Loading the `.npy` files is also I/O bound but relatively fast.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The baseline correction involves sliding window operations over the full session length for every neuron. GPU acceleration (`DEVICE = torch.device('cuda')`) mitigates this.\n\n## 6-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. This could be vectorized by pre-allocating the output array and filling in all interpolated values at once.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The number of dropped frames is typically very small, so the performance impact is negligible.\n\n## 6-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\nN/A\n\n## 6-d. The\n   161→    dataset README states dropped frames can be recovered from tstamps.npy; a dropped\n   162→    frame shows up as an inter-frame interval of ~2x the median.\n   163→\n   164→    Returns\n   165→    -------\n   166→    me   : (n_frames,) float64, NaN at imaging frames with no camera frame\n   167→    info : dict with diagnostics\n   168→    \"\"\"\n   169→    me_raw = np.load(os.path.join(session_dir, 'move_deve',\n   170→                                  'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\n   171→    n_cam = len(me_raw)\n   172→\n   173→    if n_cam > n_frames:\n   174→        # Never happens in this dataset; guard so that extra trailing camera frames\n   175→        # (acquisition stopped after the microscope) cannot shift the alignment.\n   176→        print(f'    WARNING: {session_dir}: {n_cam - n_frames} more camera frames than '\n   177→              f'imaging frames; truncating the trailing ones', flush=True)\n   178→        me_raw = me_raw[:n_frames]\n   179→        n_cam = n_frames\n   180→\n   181→    if n_cam == n_frames:\n   182→        # No frames missing: one-to-one correspondence.\n   183→        frame_idx = np.arange(n_frames)\n   184→        n_missing = 0\n   185→        max_gap = 1\n   186→    else:\n   187→        ts = np.load(os.path.join(session_dir, 'move_deve', 'tstamps.npy'))\n   188→        d = np.diff(ts)\n   189→        step = np.median(d)\n   190→        # number of *extra* frame slots spanned by each inter-frame interval\n   191→        n_steps = np.round(d / step).astype(int)\n   192→        frame_idx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(n_steps)])\n   193→        n_missing = int(frame_idx[-1] + 1 - n_cam)\n   194→        max_gap = int(n_steps.max())\n   195→        if frame_idx[-1] + 1 != n_frames:\n   196→            raise ValueError(\n   197→                f'{session_dir}: reconstructed camera frame indices span '\n   198→                f'{frame_idx[-1] + 1} frames but there are {n_frames} imaging frames')\n   199→\n   200→    me = np.full(n_frames, np.nan, dtype=np.float64)\n   201→    me[frame_idx] = me_raw\n   202→\n   203→    # motion_energy_glob[0] is identically 0 in every session: there is no frame -1 to\n   204→    # difference against, so it is an artefact rather than a measurement.\n   205→    me[0] = np.nan\n   206→\n   207→    info = {'n_camera_frames': n_cam, 'n_missing_frames': n_missing,\n   208→            'max_gap_frames': max_gap,\n   209→            'frac_valid': float(np.mean(~np.isnan(me)))}\n   210→    return me, info\n   211→\n   212→\n   213→def discretize_quantiles(x, n_quantiles=N_QUANTILES):\n   214→    \"\"\"Assign each value of `x` to one of `n_quantiles` equal-percentile bins.\n   215→\n   216→    Percentile edges are computed from `x` itself (i.e. per session).  Returns int labels\n   217→    in [0, n_quantiles-1] and the edges used.\n   218→    \"\"\"\n   219→    edges = np.percentile(x, np.arange(1, n_quantiles) * (100.0 / n_quantiles))\n   220→    labels = np.searchsorted(edges, x, side='right').astype(np.int64)\n   221→    return labels, edges\n   222→\n   223→\n   224→# ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n   225→# Per-session conversion\n   226→# ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n   227→def convert_session(session_dir, keep_neurons=None, verbose=True):\n   228→    \"\"\"Convert one session.\n   229→\n   230→    Returns a dict with the per-trial neural/input/output lists and diagnostics.\n   231→    \"\"\"\n   232→    t0 = time.time()\n   233→\n   234→    F = load_traces(session_dir)\n   235→    fs = load_fs(session_dir)\n   236→    if fs != FS:\n   237→        raise ValueError(f'{session_dir}: unexpected fs={fs}')\n   238→    n_neurons_all, n_frames = F.shape\n   239→\n   240→    if keep_neurons is not None:\n   241→        F = F[keep_neurons]\n   242→    n_neurons = F.shape[0]\n   243→    t_load = time.time() - t0\n   244→\n   245→    # ---- neural: dF/F then 10-frame binning -------------------------------------\n   246→    t1 = time.time()\n   247→    dff, baseline = f_processing(F, fs=fs, return_baseline=True)\n   248→    dff_binned = bin_mean(dff).astype(np.float32)            # (n_neurons, n_bins)\n   249→    t_neural = time.time() - t1\n   250→\n   251→    # ---- behaviour: motion energy on imaging frames, then 10-frame binning -------\n   252→    t2 = time.time()\n   253→    me_frames, me_info = motion_energy_on_imaging_frames(session_dir, n_frames)\n   254→    me_binned = bin_mean(me_frames, nan_aware=True)           # (n_bins,)\n   255→    if np.any(np.isnan(me_binned)):\n   256→        # A whole 10-frame bin without a single camera frame. Does not occur in this\n   257→        # dataset (all gaps are single frames) but handle it rather than crash.\n   258→        bad = np.isnan(me_binned)\n   259→        good = ~bad\n   260→        me_binned[bad] = np.interp(np.flatnonzero(bad), np.flatnonzero(good),\n   261→                                   me_binned[good])\n   262→        me_info['n_interpolated_bins'] = int(bad.sum())\n   263→    else:\n   264→        me_info['n_interpolated_bins'] = 0\n   265→\n   266→    me_labels, me_edges = discretize_quantiles(me_binned)\n   267→    t_behav = time.time() - t2\n   268→\n   269→    # ---- trials: consecutive non-overlapping 60 s blocks -------------------------\n   270→    n_bins = dff_binned.shape[1]\n   271→    assert me_binned.shape[0] == n_bins\n   272→    n_trials = n_bins // BINS_PER_TRIAL\n   273→    n_dropped_bins = n_bins - n_trials * BINS_PER_TRIAL\n   274→\n   275→    # time (s) elapsed from session start at the centre of each bin\n   276→    bin_centre_s = (np.arange(n_bins) * BIN_FRAMES + (BIN_FRAMES - 1) / 2.0) / fs\n   277→\n   278→    neural, inputs, outputs = [], [], []\n   279→    for k in range(n_trials):\n   280→        sl = slice(k * BINS_PER_TRIAL, (k + 1) * BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n   281→        neural.append(np.ascontiguousarray(dff_binned[:, sl]))\n   282→        inputs.append(bin_centre_s[sl][None, :].astype(np.float32))\n   283→        outputs.append(me_labels[sl][None, :].astype(np.int64))\n   284→\n   285→    out = {\n   286→        'neural': neural,\n   287→        'input': inputs,\n   288→        'output': outputs,\n   289→        'n_neurons': n_neurons,\n   290→        'n_neurons_all': n_neurons_all,\n   291→        'n_frames': n_frames,\n   292→        'n_bins': n_bins,\n   293→        'n_trials': n_trials,\n   294→        'n_dropped_bins': n_dropped_bins,\n   295→        'me_edges': me_edges,\n   296→        'me_info': me_info,\n   297→        'class_fractions': np.bincount(me_labels, minlength=N_QUANTILES) / n_bins,\n   298→        'timing': {'load': t_load, 'neural': t_neural, 'behaviour': t_behav,\n   299→                   'total': time.time() - t0},\n   300→        # kept only for --show-processing\n   301→        '_raw': {'F': F, 'baseline': baseline, 'dff': dff, 'dff_binned': dff_binned,\n   302→                 'me_frames': me_frames, 'me_binned': me_binned,\n   303→                 'me_labels': me_labels, 'bin_centre_s': bin_centre_s},\n   304→    }\n   305→    if verbose:\n   306→        print(f'    {os.path.basename(session_dir)}: {n_neurons} neurons, '\n   307→              f'{n_frames} frames -> {n_bins} bins -> {n_trials} trials | '\n   308→              f'missing camera frames: {me_info[\"n_missing_frames\"]} '\n   309→              f'({100 * (1 - me_info[\"frac_valid\"]):.2f}% of frames) | '\n   310→              f'class fractions {np.round(out[\"class_fractions\"], 3)} | '\n   311→              f'{out[\"timing\"][\"total\"]:.2f}s', flush=True)\n   312→    return out\n   313→\n   314→\n   315→def find_bad_neurons(subject_sessions):\n   316→    \"\"\"Boolean mask of tracked ROIs that are identically zero on at least one day.\n   317→\n   318→    Such ROIs fell outside the imaged FOV on that day; Suite2p returns an all-zero trace.\n   319→    They are dropped for every session of the subject so that the tracked population\n   320→    stays matched across days.\n   321→    \"\"\"\n   322→    bad = None\n   323→    for _, _, sess_dir in subject_sessions:\n   324→        F = np.load(os.path.join(sess_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0', 'F.npy'), mmap_mode='r')\n   325→        b = np.asarray(F).std(axis=1) == 0\n   326→        bad = b if bad is None else (bad | b)\n   327→    return bad\n   328→\n   329→\n   330→# ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n   331→# Plotting of the processing steps\n   332→# ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n   333→def plot_processing(session_id, res, outdir='/app'):\n   334→    import matplotlib\n   335→    matplotlib.use('Agg')\n   336→    import matplotlib.pyplot as plt\n   337→\n   338→    raw = res['_raw']\n   339→    F, base, dff = raw['F'], raw['baseline'], raw['dff']\n   340→    dffb, meb, lab = raw['dff_binned'], raw['me_binned'], raw['me_labels']\n   341→    tb = raw['bin_centre_s']\n   342→    tf = np.arange(F.shape[1]) / FS\n   343→\n   344→    fig, ax = plt.subplots(7, 1, figsize=(17, 21))\n   345→\n   346→    # 1) raw F + baseline for 3 example neurons\n   347→    ex = np.linspace(0, F.shape[0] - 1, 3).astype(int)\n   348→    for i, n in enumerate(ex):\n   349→        ax[0].plot(tf, F[n] + i * np.ptp(F[ex]), lw=0.4, color='C0',\n   350→                   label='raw F' if i == 0 else None)\n   351→        ax[0].plot(tf, base[n] + i * np.ptp(F[ex]), lw=1.0, color='C3',\n   352→                   label='maximin baseline' if i == 0 else None)\n   353→    ax[0].set_title(f'{session_id} — 1) raw fluorescence and Suite2p maximin baseline '\n   354→                    f'(neurons {ex})')\n   355→    ax[0].set_ylabel('F (a.u.)'); ax[0].legend(loc='upper right')\n   356→\n   357→    # 2) dF/F for the same neurons\n   358→    for i, n in enumerate(ex):\n   359→        ax[1].plot(tf, dff[n] + i * np.ptp(dff[ex]), lw=0.4, color='C0',\n   360→                   label='dF/F (30 Hz)' if i == 0 else None)\n   361→        ax[1].plot(tb, dffb[n] + i * np.ptp(dff[ex]), lw=0.8, color='C1',\n   362→                   label='dF/F binned (3 Hz)' if i == 0 else None)\n   363→    ax[1].set_title('2) baseline-subtracted dF/F, before and after 10-frame binning')\n   364→    ax[1].set_ylabel('dF/F (a.u.)'); ax[1].legend(loc='upper right')\n   365→\n   366→    # 3) binned raster + motion energy overlay\n   367→    ax[2].imshow(dffb, aspect='auto', cmap='gray_r',\n   368→                 vmin=0, vmax=np.percentile(dffb, 99),\n   369→                 extent=[tb[0], tb[-1], dffb.shape[0], 0])\n   370→    ax[2].set_title('3) binned dF/F raster (all neurons)')\n   371→    ax[2].set_ylabel('neuron')\n   372→\n   373→    # 4) motion energy at frame rate and binned\n   374→    ax[3].plot(tf, raw['me_frames'], lw=0.3, color='0.6', label='motion energy (30 Hz)')\n   375→    ax[3].plot(tb, meb, lw=0.8, color='C2', label='motion energy binned (3 Hz)')\n   376→    ax[3].set_yscale('symlog')\n   377→    ax[3].set_title('4) motion energy on the imaging-frame grid (NaN = dropped camera frame)')\n   378→    ax[3].set_ylabel('motion energy'); ax[3].legend(loc='upper right')\n   379→\n   380→    # 5) discretisation\n   381→    ax[4].plot(tb, meb, lw=0.8, color='C2', label='binned motion energy')\n   382→    for e in res['me_edges']:\n   383→        ax[4].axhline(e, color='k', ls='--', lw=0.8)\n   384→    ax[4].set_yscale('symlog')\n   385→    ax4b = ax[4].twinx()\n   386→    ax4b.plot(tb, lab, lw=0.6, color='C3', alpha=0.7)\n   387→    ax4b.set_ylabel('quintile label', color='C3')\n   388→    ax[4].set_title('5) quintile discretisation (dashed = 20/40/60/80th percentile of the session)')\n   389→    ax[4].set_ylabel('motion energy'); ax[4].legend(loc='upper right')\n   390→\n   391→    # 6) class histogram\n   392→    ax[5].bar(np.arange(N_QUANTILES), res['class_fractions'])\n   393→    ax[5].axhline(1.0 / N_QUANTILES, color='k', ls='--')\n   394→    ax[5].set_title('6) class fractions (dashed = 1/5)')\n   395→    ax[5].set_xlabel('motion energy quintile'); ax[5].set_ylabel('fraction of bins')\n   396→\n   397→    # 7) reassembled trials: check for temporal alignment / no gaps at trial borders\n   398→    t_cat = np.concatenate([res['input'][k][0] for k in range(res['n_trials'])])\n   399→    o_cat = np.concatenate([res['output'][k][0] for k in range(res['n_trials'])])\n   400→    n_cat = np.concatenate([res['neural'][k][ex[0]] for k in range(res['n_trials'])])\n   401→    ax[6].plot(t_cat, o_cat, lw=0.6, color='C3', label='output (from trials)')\n   402→    ax[6].plot(tb[:len(t_cat)], lab[:len(t_cat)] + 0.15, lw=0.6, color='k', ls=':',\n   403→               label='output (session-level, +0.15 offset)')\n   404→    ax6b = ax[6].twinx()\n   405→    ax6b.plot(t_cat, n_cat, lw=0.4, color='C0', alpha=0.6, label='neural (from trials)')\n   406→    ax6b.set_ylabel('dF/F neuron %d' % ex[0], color='C0')\n   407→    for k in range(res['n_trials']):\n   408→        ax[6].axvline(t_cat[k * BINS_PER_TRIAL], color='0.8', lw=0.5)\n   409→    ax[6].set_title('7) trials re-concatenated: time axis continuous across trial borders '\n   410→                    '(grey lines) and output identical to the session-level trace')\n   411→    ax[6].set_xlabel('time from session start (s)'); ax[6].set_ylabel('quintile')\n   412→    ax[6].legend(loc='upper right')\n   413→\n   414→    for a in ax[:6]:\n   415→        a.set_xlabel('time from session start (s)')\n   416→\n   417→    fig.tight_layout()\n   418→    path = os.path.join(outdir, f'processing_{session_id}.png')\n   419→    fig.savefig(path, dpi=110)\n   420→    plt.close(fig)\n   421→    print(f'    saved {path}', flush=True)\n   422→\n   423→\n   424→# ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n   425→# Main\n   426→# ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n   427→def main():\n   428→    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__,\n   429→                                 formatter_class=argparse.RawDescriptionHelpFormatter)\n   430→    ap.add_argument('outfile', type=str, help='output pickle path')\n   431→    g = ap.add_mutually_exclusive_group()\n   432→    g.add_argument('--full', action='store_true', help='process all sessions (default)')\n   433→    g.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true', help='process only 2 sessions')\n   434→    ap.add_argument('--show-processing', action='store_true',\n   435→                    help='plot every processing step for up to 2 sessions')\n   436→    args = ap.parse_args()\n   437→\n   438→    t_start = time.time()\n   439→    all_sessions = list_sessions()\n   440→    print(f'Found {len(all_sessions)} sessions from '\n   441→          f'{len(set(s[0] for s in all_sessions))} subjects.', flush=True)\n   442→\n   443→    if args.sample:\n   444→        # one 20-min session (jm031) and one 30-min session (jm039) so that both\n   445→        # session lengths and a session with dropped camera frames are exercised\n   446→        wanted = [('jm031', '2023-10-22_a'), ('jm039', '2024-05-06_a')]\n   447→        sessions = [s for s in all_sessions if (s[0], s[1]) in wanted]\n   448→        print(f'--sample: processing {len(sessions)} sessions: '\n   449→              f'{[s[0] + \"/\" + s[1] for s in sessions]}', flush=True)\n   450→    else:\n   451→        sessions = all_sessions\n   452→\n   453→    # neurons to drop, computed per subject over ALL of that subject's sessions\n   454→    subjects_used = sorted(set(s[0] for s in sessions))\n   455→    keep_masks = {}\n   456→    for subject in subjects_used:\n   457→        subj_sessions = [s for s in all_sessions if s[0] == subject]\n   458→        bad = find_bad_neurons(subj_sessions)\n   459→        keep_masks[subject] = ~bad\n   460→        if bad.any():\n   461→            print(f'  {subject}: dropping {bad.sum()} of {len(bad)} tracked ROIs with an '\n   462→                  f'all-zero trace on >=1 day (indices {np.flatnonzero(bad).tolist()})',\n   463→                  flush=True)\n   464→\n   465→    data = {\n   466→        'neural': [], 'input': [], 'output': [],\n   467→        'subjects': [], 'subject_idx': [],\n   468→        'brain_regions': [BRAIN_REGION], 'brain_region_idx': [],\n   469→        'input_names': ['time_from_session_start_s'],\n   470→        'output_names': ['motion_energy_quintile'],\n   471→        'output_values': [[f'q{i + 1}' for i in range(N_QUANTILES)]],\n   472→        'metadata': {},\n   473→    }\n   474→    subject_list = sorted(SUBJECT_INFO.keys())\n   475→    session_info = []\n   476→    n_plotted = 0\n   477→\n   478→    for (subject, sess_name, sess_dir) in sessions:\n   479→        res = convert_session(sess_dir, keep_neurons=keep_masks[subject])\n   480→\n   481→        data['neural'].append(res['neural'])\n   482→        data['input'].append(res['input'])\n   483→        data['output'].append(res['output'])\n   484→        data['subject_idx'].append(subject_list.index(subject))\n   485→        data['brain_region_idx'].append(np.zeros(res['n_neurons'], dtype=np.int64))\n   486→\n   487→        # postnatal day: first session of the subject is SUBJECT_INFO[subject][1],\n   488→        # recordings are on consecutive days\n   489→        subj_sessions = [s[1] for s in all_sessions if s[0] == subject]\n   490→        day_index = subj_sessions.index(sess_name)\n   491→        session_info.append({\n   492→            'subject': subject,\n   493→            'paper_mouse': SUBJECT_INFO[subject][0],\n   494→            'session': sess_name,\n   495→            'date': sess_name[:10],\n   496→            'day_index': day_index,\n   497→            'postnatal_day': SUBJECT_INFO[subject][1] + day_index,\n   498→            'n_neurons': res['n_neurons'],\n   499→            'n_neurons_before_curation': res['n_neurons_all'],\n   500→            'n_frames': res['n_frames'],\n   501→            'n_bins': res['n_bins'],\n   502→            'n_trials': res['n_trials'],\n   503→            'duration_s': res['n_frames'] / FS,\n   504→            'motion_energy_quintile_edges': res['me_edges'].tolist(),\n   505→            'class_fractions': res['class_fractions'].tolist(),\n   506→            'n_missing_camera_frames': res['me_info']['n_missing_frames'],\n   507→            'frac_frames_with_behaviour': res['me_info']['frac_valid'],\n   508→        })\n   509→\n   510→        if args.show_processing and n_plotted < 2:\n   511→            plot_processing(f'{subject}_{sess_name}', res)\n   512→            n_plotted += 1\n   513→        res.pop('_raw')\n   514→\n   515→    data['subjects'] = subject_list\n   516→    data['subject_idx'] = np.array(data['subject_idx'], dtype=np.int64)\n   517→\n   518→    n_sessions = len(data['neural'])\n   519→    n_trials = sum(len(s) for s in data['neural'])\n   520→    data['metadata'] = {\n   521→        'task_description': (\n   522→            'Spontaneous activity in mouse barrel cortex (S1, layer 2/3) during the '\n   523→            'second postnatal week (P7-P14). Head-fixed pups sat on a non-motorised '\n   524→            'treadmill in the dark under sensory-minimised conditions; there was no task '\n   525→            'and no stimulus. Neural data are 2-photon GCaMP8m calcium traces (dF/F) of '\n   526→            'neurons tracked across all recording days of a mouse with Track2p. The '\n   527→            'decoder predicts the animal\\'s behavioural state, quantified as the motion '\n   528→            'energy of simultaneous 30 Hz videography (sum of squared pixel-wise '\n   529→            'differences between consecutive video frames), discretised into five '\n   530→            'equal-percentile (quintile) bins computed separately for each session. The '\n   531→            'decoder additionally receives the time elapsed since the start of the '\n   532→            'session.'),\n   533→        'time_bin_size': BIN_SIZE_MS,\n   534→        'temporal_alignment_event': (\n   535→            'Start of the 60 s trial. Recordings are continuous spontaneous activity '\n   536→            'with no task events, so each session is simply cut into consecutive, '\n   537→            'non-overlapping 60 s blocks starting at the first imaging frame.'),\n   538→        'off_start': 0.0,\n   539→        'off_end': TRIAL_LEN_S,\n   540→        'sampling_rate_hz': FS,\n   541→        'bin_frames': BIN_FRAMES,\n   542→        'trial_duration_s': TRIAL_LEN_S,\n   543→        'bins_per_trial': BINS_PER_TRIAL,\n   544→        'neural_signal': (\n   545→            'dF/F = F - maximin baseline, computed exactly as in the reference code '\n   546→            '(track2p/gui/data_management.py:F_processing): no neuropil subtraction '\n   547→            '(neucoeff=0), gaussian_filter sigma=10 frames along time, minimum_filter1d '\n   548→            'then maximum_filter1d with a 60 s window; then averaged in non-overlapping '\n   549→            'bins of 10 frames (Methods: \"averaging in bins of 10 consecutive '\n   550→            'timestamps\").'),\n   551→        'neuron_curation': (\n   552→            'The released files already contain only the ROIs that Suite2p classified as '\n   553→            'cells (probability > 0.5) AND that Track2p tracked across all recording days '\n   554→            'of that mouse; rows are matched across days. Additionally, ROIs whose trace '\n   555→            'is identically zero on at least one day of the mouse (outside the imaged FOV '\n   556→            'that day) were removed from every session of that mouse.'),\n   557→        'input_units': ['s'],\n   558→        'input_description': [\n   559→            'Time elapsed from the first imaging frame of the session, in seconds, at the '\n   560→            'centre of each 333.33 ms bin. Continuous across trial boundaries.'],\n   561→        'output_description': [\n   562→            'Motion energy (videography), binned to 333.33 ms and discretised into five '\n   563→            'equal-percentile bins whose edges are the 20/40/60/80th percentiles of that '\n   564→            'session. 0 = least motion, 4 = most motion.'],\n   565→        'brain_region_description': {\n   566→            'S1': 'Primary somatosensory (barrel) cortex, layer 2/3, 100-200 um deep, '\n   567→                  '720x720 um FOV'},\n   568→        'species': 'Mus musculus (GAD67-Cre pups, P7-P14)',\n   569→        'paper': ('Majnik J, Mantez M, Zangila S, Bugeon S, Guignard L, Platel J-C, '\n   570→                  'Cossart R (2025) Longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the '\n   571→                  'same cells in the developing brain using Track2p. eLife 14:RP107540'),\n   572→        'n_sessions': n_sessions,\n   573→        'n_trials': n_trials,\n   574→        'session_info': session_info,\n   575→    }\n   576→\n   577→    # ---------------------- sanity checks ----------------------------------------\n   578→    print('\\nRunning sanity checks ...', flush=True)\n   579→    assert len(data['input']) == n_sessions and len(data['output']) == n_sessions\n   580→    assert len(data['subject_idx']) == n_sessions\n   581→    assert len(data['brain_region_idx']) == n_sessions\n   582→    for s in range(n_sessions):\n   583→        nt = len(data['neural'][s])\n   584→        assert nt >= 2, f'session {s} has only {nt} trials'\n   585→        assert len(data['input'][s]) == nt and len(data['output'][s]) == nt\n   586→        nneur = data['neural'][s][0].shape[0]\n   587→        assert len(data['brain_region_idx'][s]) == nneur\n   588→        for k in range(nt):\n   589→            assert data['neural'][s][k].shape == (nneur, BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n   590→            assert data['input'][s][k].shape == (1, BINS_PER_TRIAL)\n   591→
